#10810. How to Use Developer Time Tracking Data to Improve Sprint Planning and Estimates

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sprint estimates usually fail because development teams heavily rely on memory and assumptions instead of evidence. They assume similar tasks will take the same amount of time. Based on these half-baked assumptions, most managers plan around ideal sprint working hours. The backlog then fills with commitments that look realistic on paper but often end up delayed.

Actual development rarely follows that plan. There are always unexpected bugs and task dependencies that consume more time than teams account for in their estimates. Even the buffer time set aside for unexpected work may not be enough. This causes tasks to take longer than expected and pushes sprint commitments behind schedule.

Developer time tracking helps teams make better estimates with historical data. It shows how long previous tasks actually took, which provides managers with an evidence-based reference when they are planning similar work.

Reviewing this data before committing to a sprint scope turns estimation from guesswork into an evidence-based process.

Historical time data offers deeper insights, depending on how you collect and analyze it. Most importantly, it explains why planned work took longer than expected.

Before sprint planning, the following records should be reviewed.

Completed task duration: Check the time spent on finished tasks. Managers should compare similar work to understand typical development effort.

Actual versus estimated hours: Management should observe and identify differences between planned and recorded hours. If there are repeated delays, to some extent, it indicates inaccurate estimates.

Carryover work: Review unfinished tasks from previous sprints. Determine whether poor estimates or external delays caused the spillover..

Interruptions: Measure time spent on urgent requests and production issues. These activities reduce the development capacity that you had planned earlier.

Meetings and ceremonies: Review the time being spent on planning sessions and sprint reviews.

Bug-fix time: Examine hours spent resolving defects. You can also compare it to the time for developing new features.

Blocked tasks: Managers should account for the tasks delayed by missing approvals or technical dependencies.

Capacity differences: Compare team availability across sprints. Include leave and staffing changes in that time as well.

These records provide the most value when reviewed together. For example, a task may finish three days late even if the development work stayed within its estimated hours. The actual issue could be an external dependency.

Teams should understand these differences before using historical data to estimate future work.

Historical records provide a useful starting point for estimating new tasks. However, teams must interpret the data rather than simply copy previous estimates.

The following approaches can improve estimation accuracy.

The first step is to consider completed tasks that resemble upcoming work. Managers should compare their requirements and technical complexity. Moreover, the development environment should be considered.

For example, suppose a team needs to estimate a new API integration. Three previous integrations required 12, 15, and 18 hours of active work.

The average is 15 hours. This gives developers a reference point for the new estimate.

However, the average doesn't tell the entire story. The new integration may involve unfamiliar authentication methods or more complicated testing.

Review the reasons behind differences in previous task durations. Check whether developers encountered unexpected bugs or unclear requirements.

As a project manager, I'd consider these details before estimating for the next project. In my experience, they provide more context than an average alone.

Inaccurate estimates can happen; it doesn’t mean that there is a definite planning problem. However, repeated overruns across similar tasks may reveal an underlying issue.

Research suggests the pattern is widespread. A review of software estimation surveys reports that 60–80% of software projects encounter effort overruns, with the average overrun falling between 30% and 40%.

Start by calculating the difference between estimated and actual effort. Here is the formula for it.

Estimation variance (%) = [(Actual hours − Estimated hours) ÷ Estimated hours] × 100

Suppose a task was estimated at 10 hours but needed 14 hours. The actual effort exceeded the estimate by 40%.

You can compare the results across similar tasks.

For example, database migrations may repeatedly require more time than expected. This means that the team might be overlooking data validation or additional testing. Once the cause is clear, adjust future estimates accordingly.

Avoid increasing every estimate by the same percentage. Different types of work involve different risks. Teams can also break large tasks into smaller parts. This makes hidden development activities easier to identify.

The aim is to correct recurring estimation errors without unnecessarily increasing the entire sprint backlog.

Task duration doesn't always reflect the hours spent developing a feature.

A study of developer workdays by Meyer and colleagues, which tracked 2,197 hours of activity across 20 developers, found that coding accounted for around 21% of the typical day. Collaborative work such as meetings and email took up roughly 24%.

Consider a developer who starts a feature on Monday and finishes it on Wednesday. The task took three days to complete. This is pretty straightforward, and the estimation could be based on it; however, the developer may have spent several hours in meetings. Other than that, urgent support requests could also have consumed additional time.

The actual development effort could be much lower than three full working days. This distinction matters during estimation.

Teams should record focused development time separately from interruptions. They should also track waiting periods caused by blocked tasks. A more appropriate approach would be to use active development hours to estimate similar future work. They should also account for recurring interruptions when calculating sprint capacity.

For example, a task may regularly wait for security approval. Adding more development hours to its estimate won't resolve that delay.

Instead, the team should recognize the dependency and consider its effect on delivery.

If managers are able to separate these activities, it will help create better estimates without confusing effort with elapsed time.

A precise estimate can create false confidence when a task involves substantial uncertainty.

Suppose similar tasks previously required between 12 and 20 hours. If you estimate the next task at exactly 14 hours, it will overlook important differences.

A range of 12–20 hours provides a clearer picture of the possible effort. Developers can then discuss what might push the task toward either end of the range.

For example, the lower estimate can be directly applied to integration, and the upper estimate might be considered for unfamiliar code or additional testing.

In such scenarios, if the requirements stay unclear, consider a short technical investigation before finalizing the estimate. This can help identify dependencies and reduce uncertainty.

However, estimate ranges should still support practical planning decisions. Teams need to determine how much capacity to reserve for the work.

Choose a planning assumption that reflects the task's risk and available capacity.

As developers learn more about the task, they can refine the estimate.

Historical time data should affect how much work a team accepts during sprint planning. Better estimates offer little value if teams continue making unrealistic commitments.

If several sprints end with unfinished tasks, it is best that you first review why the team missed its commitments in the first place. Once you have identified the causes, use those findings to reduce the next sprint's scope.

Teams should prioritize work that supports the sprint goal. Moreover, avoid adding extra tasks just because they appear manageable during planning.

Production incidents and urgent requests can disrupt planned development. To get more reliable estimations, review how much time these activities consumed in previous sprints. Then reserve an appropriate amount of capacity.

Historical estimates become less reliable in case unfamiliar technology or additional dependencies are involved. It is best to break complex tasks into smaller components where possible. This helps developers assess the work more accurately.

Nominal capacity represents scheduled working hours, whereas actual capacity reflects the time available after accounting for other commitments.

The Scrum Guide identifies previous performance and upcoming capacity as important inputs to sprint forecasting.

When you are selecting backlog items, consider both. Additionally, avoid committing every available hour or deducting the same interruption twice.

Historical time data is useful for sprint planning. Most development teams already use project management tools that include basic time tracking features.

However, these tools don't always break down how developers spend their working hours. For clearer time data, a feature-specific time tracker is more useful because it provides more details about tasks and workloads.

TimeBee is one such developer time tracking tool I've used. It tracks working hours and time spent on projects and tasks. It also provides attendance records and workforce activity reports.

With this data, teams can review how much time previous tasks required. For example, developers can record hours spent building a feature. They can also track debugging and revisions separately. Later, managers can compare these records with the original estimates. This helps them plan similar work more accurately.

TimeBee also tracks website and application activity. These reports can help teams understand how working hours are distributed.

However, not all activities reflect actual development effort. Sometimes a developer just spends time reviewing documentation or discussing technical problems. Such work may not always appear in TimeBee as active computer use.

Apart from that, attendance, leave, and overtime records from TimeBee can help managers calculate sprint capacity. They can review developer availability before deciding how much work to include in the next sprint.

One thing to remember is that developer time tracking should help teams understand workload patterns. It shouldn't be used to monitor every minute of a developer's day. Management should be careful that the aim is to improve estimates and make more realistic sprint commitments.

Sprint retrospectives should include a review of how the planning is going. If you have made a plan, it doesn’t mean you cannot make changes to it. During sprints, keep comparing original estimates with actual task hours. Track your unfinished work for recurring delays.

Ask developers what caused significant differences. Some tasks may have required additional testing. Others may have been delayed by dependencies. Select one specific improvement for the next sprint.

Not every estimation difference indicates a planning failure. Unexpected technical challenges are part of software development.

The objective is to improve forecasts over time rather than eliminate every variance.

Historical time data provides a more stable foundation for sprint planning. Developer time tracking helps teams understand what is actually happening within the sprint rather than creating unnecessary pressure for them.

Other than that, the data also reveals how interruptions affect available development hours.

However, time data should guide planning rather than dictate it.

1. What is the difference between sprint velocity and time-based capacity planning?

Sprint velocity measures how much work a team has completed during a sprint. It is usually measured in story points. Contrary to this, time-based capacity planning calculates the hours available for planned work. It accounts for meetings and other responsibilities.

Velocity helps predict future delivery based on past performance. Capacity planning shows whether the team has enough time to meet its commitments.

2. Which time tracking metrics are most useful for improving Agile sprint forecasts? The most useful metrics include estimated versus actual hours, task completion time, and unplanned work.

Bug-fix time, rework, and blocked tasks also provide valuable insights. Teams should also review available capacity across previous sprints.

These metrics help sort out recurring delays and estimation errors. They allow teams to forecast workloads more accurately and make realistic sprint commitments.

**3. How can development teams improve estimates when historical time tracking data is incomplete? **

Teams can start with available task records and completed sprint backlogs. Developer feedback can help fill information gaps. Similar tasks from previous projects may also provide useful estimates.

When data is limited, teams should use estimate ranges instead of fixed hours. Consistent time tracking in future sprints will gradually provide more reliable planning data.

4. How can time tracking data help balance workloads across developers during sprint planning?

Time tracking data shows how much time developers spend on assigned tasks and other responsibilities. Managers can review these records to identify uneven workloads. They can then distribute upcoming tasks based on available capacity.

Technical expertise and task complexity should also influence assignments. This helps prevent over commitment and supports a more balanced workload across the development team.

Twitch star Ibai Llanos left a small GTA 6 streamer in tears after encouraging his followers to tune into his broadcast, which had no viewers.

The launch of GTA 6 on November 19 saw countless content creators flock to Twitch to stream Rockstar’s long-awaited game, with some of the platform’s biggest personalities attracting enormous audiences.

However, Spanish streaming sensation Ibai Llanos decided to turn his attention to the other end of the spectrum, searching for a GTA 6 streamer with no viewers in the hopes of giving them a boost.

As reported by Meristation, Ibai discovered a Dominican streamer named Dicrim_RD69, who was broadcasting his playthrough of GTA 6 to an audience of zero people.

After joining the broadcast himself, Ibai left a simple “Hello” in the chat before encouraging his followers on social media to visit the channel, follow the streamer, and keep him company.

His plan worked, with Dicrim’s audience skyrocketing to 3,243 concurrent viewers within hours. Initially laughing in disbelief at his sudden popularity, Dicrim became overwhelmed with emotion after discovering Ibai was responsible.

“Thank you. You guys have brought me to tears,” he said. “Ibai, I love you. I’ve been a fan of Ibai for a long time.”

The pair subsequently spoke over a video call, during which Dicrim revealed that he had been streaming for more than a year without attracting much of an audience.

“When you don’t have someone to talk to, to communicate with, you don’t feel the same. You feel bad,” he explained, admitting he had been feeling particularly upset the previous night.

Ibai reassured him by recalling his own beginnings as a content creator, revealing that he and his cousin used to stream basketball commentary to zero viewers when he was just 14 years old.

“If it makes you happy, keep doing it,” Ibai encouraged him. Dicrim, who explained that his family couldn’t afford video games when he was younger, thanked Ibai for the gesture. “You made me the happiest man in the world,” he said.

#10802. How to Improve Email Deliverability Rates

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You wrote a good email. You hit send. Then half your list never saw it, because it went straight to spam.

That is a deliverability problem, and it costs more than most teams think. Every email that misses the inbox is a lead you paid for and never reached.

The good part is that deliverability is not luck. Mailbox providers like Gmail, Yahoo and Outlook judge you on a short list of things: who you are, who you send to, and how people react. Fix those and your inbox placement goes up.

This guide covers 10 email deliverability best practices in the order you should fix them, starting with SPF and ending with sender reputation.

Email deliverability is the share of your emails that land in the inbox instead of the spam folder or nowhere at all.

People often mix it up with delivery rate. They are not the same thing.

You can have a 99% delivery rate and still have most of your emails sitting in spam. That is why your email tool can say "delivered" while your open rate keeps dropping.

The big mailbox providers stopped treating authentication as optional.

Microsoft followed on May 5, 2025. If you send more than 5,000 emails a day to Outlook.com, Hotmail or Live addresses, you need all three records in place or your mail goes to junk.

Send less than 5,000 a day? Follow the rules anyway. The same checks decide inbox placement for small senders too, just without the public warning.

SPF (Sender Policy Framework) is a TXT record in your DNS. It lists the servers allowed to send email from your domain.

When your email arrives, the receiving server checks that list. If the sending server is not on it, the email looks suspicious.

DKIM (DomainKeys Identified Mail) adds a digital signature to each email. The receiving server uses a public key in your DNS to check two things: the email came from you, and nobody changed it on the way.

Your email provider gives you the DKIM record. You paste it into your DNS and switch signing on.

DKIM also survives forwarding, which SPF does not. If you can only get one of the two perfect, get DKIM right.

DMARC ties SPF and DKIM together. It tells mailbox providers what to do when an email fails both checks, and it sends you reports on who is sending mail under your domain name.

p=none means "take no action, just send me reports." It is the minimum Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft accept from bulk senders.

Don't stop there. Read the reports for a few weeks, fix any real sender that is failing, then move up:

One more thing: DMARC needs alignment. The domain in your From address has to match the domain used in SPF or DKIM. Plenty of senders have all three records and still fail DMARC because their email tool signs with its own domain.

If you'd rather not handle this in-house, you can also hire a B2B cold email marketing agency such as IntentSignal, Martal or Belkins. A good agency will set up the domains, mailboxes, warm-up and SPF, DKIM and DMARC records for you, and keep an eye on deliverability while the campaigns run.

A new domain has no history. If it sends 10,000 emails on day one, it looks exactly like a spammer who just bought a domain.

Warm-up means starting small and growing a little each day, so mailbox providers can watch how people react to you.

Send to your most active contacts first. Their opens and replies are what build trust early. If bounces or complaints jump, hold at that volume until they settle.

And if you run cold outreach, do it from a separate domain or subdomain. That way a bad campaign can't hurt the domain your invoices and password resets come from.

A bigger list is not a better list. Dead addresses and people who stopped caring pull your numbers down.

Never buy a list. Bought lists are full of spam traps, which are addresses that exist only to catch senders who email people without permission. Hitting a few of them can get your domain blocklisted.

Yes, you lose some sign-ups. What you keep is real people with working inboxes who actually want your emails. That means fewer bounces, fewer complaints and better opens from day one.

It also keeps bots and fake addresses out of your forms, which matters a lot if your sign-up form is public.

Most email platforms add these for you. Check that yours does.

These two numbers tell you the most about where you stand.

The complaint figures come from Gmail's own rules. The bounce figures are the working limits most email platforms use.

A 0.3% complaint rate is only 3 complaints per 1,000 emails. It does not take much to cross that line.

If complaints are climbing, ask three questions:

Most complaint problems come from one of those three, not from the email itself.

Spam filters got smarter. A single word like "free" won't sink you anymore. Patterns will.

Replies help a lot. When someone replies, the mailbox provider sees a real conversation. Ask a question in your welcome email and you'll get some.

Sender reputation is the score mailbox providers give your domain and IP. Everything above feeds into it. A good score means inbox. A bad one means spam, no matter how good the email is.

You can't see the exact score, but these free tools get you close:

Check them weekly, and right after any big campaign.

A few habits protect your reputation over time:

Aim for 95% or higher inbox placement. Below 90% means something needs fixing, usually authentication or list quality.

The common causes are missing or broken SPF, DKIM or DMARC records, a high complaint rate, an old list with lots of dead addresses, or a new domain that sent too much too soon. Check authentication first because it is the fastest to fix.

DNS fixes like SPF and DKIM take effect within a day or two. Reputation is slower. Expect two to six weeks of steady, clean sending before inbox placement recovers.

Yes. The 5,000 mark is where Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft start enforcing openly. Small senders are still judged on the same signals, and Gmail asks every sender for at least SPF or DKIM.

Only if you send enough to keep it warm, roughly 100,000 emails a month or more. Below that, a good shared IP from a trusted provider usually performs better.

Sender reputation covers both your IP address and your domain. Domain reputation is the part tied to your domain name, and it follows you even if you switch email providers. Gmail leans on domain reputation more than IP.

If you only have an hour this week, spend it on SPF, DKIM and DMARC. Nothing else works until those pass.

After that, clean your list and keep an eye on complaints. Send emails people asked for, to people who still want them, from a domain that proves who it is. Do that for a few weeks and your emails will land in the inbox again.

#10794. How to Use IP Geolocation to Stop Bots and Fraud (Without Hurting UX)

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Several methods can help you deal with online threats and strengthen security. All are effective, but each has a trade-off. For example, the harder you make it for bots to affect your website’s performance, the more likely you are to frustrate real customers.

Those of you who browse online daily know that credential stuffing, fake signups & payment fraud keep growing. To prevent fraud, businesses install CAPTCHAs & hard blocks that treat bots and legitimate users the same, diverting them to your competitors.

IP geolocation maps an IP address to a physical location & network details, letting you spot suspicious traffic before it reaches sensitive actions. Used well, it’s an invisible, low-friction risk signal. In this guide, you will learn how to flag high-risk activity and apply soft geo-fencing with step-up authentication.

Today, an IP lookup returns far more than a country & city. It can also reveal several other parameters, like

Each of these fields acts as a clue telling who is really behind a request.

This data powers anomaly detection. You can start by building a baseline for normal behavior for each user with aspects like

Then, you can watch for deviations from that pattern. An attacker won’t look like the account’s real owner, so you'll catch them.

Geolocation makes several common attack patterns easy to spot:

The first is traffic bursts from data center ASNs. These are bots, as real customers don’t browse from cloud servers

Many accounts created from one IP or subnet. This is a clear sign of fake signups

Impossible travel, such as logins from two distant locations within minutes

Timezone vs. IP mismatch. A device’s clock disagrees with its apparent location.

Note: Geolocation is just a signal, not a verdict. VPNs, travel, & mobile carriers can all create false alarms, so it works best when combined with other signals. That’s exactly why a proportional, soft response beats a hard block.

In short, IP geolocation turns a bare IP address into context, helping you separate real users from automated or fraudulent traffic.

Not every IP geolocation anomaly deserves the same response. The simplest way to stay consistent is to define a clear risk-signal framework.

That can be a short list of checks. Each check can have a weight that reflects how strongly it suggests fraud.

With risk scoring, you can turn these checks into a decision. You just have to assign each signal a numeric weight.

For example, medium can be 10 & 30 can be high.

Now, you need to add up every signal that comes up & map the total to thresholds like

A single medium signal passes quietly, but several can trigger action.

If you treat this as an automatic block, it would punish legitimate customers. Instead, let it raise the score & you take action only if it stacks with other signals, like a hosting IP or a new device.

Now that you know how to quantify the risk, here is how you can protect yourself without damaging UX.

Before soft, let us learn about hard blocking. This is where you completely deny or permanently ban traffic from specific geographic regions.

Now, hard blocking by country may look effective, but it can backfire quickly. Here is how.

In the worst case, determined bot operators simply rotate to IPs in allowed regions, so the only people you stop are your real customers.

This is where the balance between security and user experience becomes critical. As the digital strategist and Founder at LawRank, Mariano Rodriguez emphasizes, "Your website's security should be a silent guardian, not a roadblock. If your fraud prevention creates friction, you aren't just stopping bots—you're turning away real clients. True digital trust is built when backend protections are seamless and invisible to the legitimate user."

Now, with soft geo-fencing, you can respond to risk proportionally instead of making a simple allow-or-deny call. Instead of totally banning the users, you adjust the level of friction to match the risk score from the previous section:

**Note: **For soft geo-fencing, passkeys are safest because they are phishing-resistant. Only a legitimate user can take the biometric tap and pass.

Before we start, let’s understand Express.js middleware. An Express.js middleware for Geolocation Risk Scores is a custom or 3rd party function that intercepts the incoming HTTP requests, extracts the client's IP address, fetches a security risk profile based on their geographic location & attaches that data to the request object (req) before it reaches your route handlers.

Here is how you can use it for fraud prevention and bot mitigation:

Note: You can make it stronger by adding velocity checks (too many attempts from one IP or account) & device fingerprints.

From a broader angle, IP geolocation isn't the final verdict. It is just a signal. But with this signal, you can score each request, respond in proportion to the risk & save hard blocks for clear abuse. This increases the friction for bots and keeps the flow easy for legitimate users.

However, if you look around The Rusty Anchor and other bars around the state of Leonida, you will notice that darts is missing. Plus, there is the Kingpin bowling alley in Port Gellhorn, but you can’t relive your days as Niko and Roman, tossing strikes down the lanes.

“The darts one is a contentious one,” Nelson said. “Even we… you hear they have unlimited money or unlimited time, even we do not and so sometimes you just have to admit we are not going to be able to make this to the level we feel we need to.”

The Rockstar North co-studio head added that pool has “proper physics” and is a “game in its own right.”

#10787. AI breakthrough cuts energy use by 100x while boosting accuracy

Artificial intelligence is consuming enormous amounts of electricity in the United States. According to the International Energy Agency, AI systems and data centers used about 415 terawatt hours of power in 2024. That accounts for more than 10% of the country's total electricity production, and demand is projected to double by 2030.

This rapid growth has raised concerns about sustainability. In response, researchers at a School of Engineering have created a proof-of-concept AI system designed to be far more efficient. Their approach could reduce energy use by up to 100 times while also improving performance on tasks.

A Hybrid Approach Called Neuro-Symbolic AI

The research comes from the laboratory of Matthias Scheutz, Karol Family Applied Technology Professor. His team is developing neuro-symbolic AI, which combines traditional neural networks with symbolic reasoning. This method mirrors how people approach problems by breaking them into steps and categories.

The work will be presented at the International Conference of Robotics and Automation in Vienna in May and will appear in the conference proceedings.

Teaching Robots to See, Understand, and Act

Unlike familiar large language models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT and Gemini, the team focuses on AI systems used in robotics. These systems are known as visual-language-action (VLA) models. They extend LLM capabilities by incorporating vision and physical movement.

VLA models take in visual data from cameras and instructions from language, then translate that information into real-world actions. For example, they can control a robot's wheels, arms, or fingers to complete a task.

Why Traditional AI Struggles With Simple Tasks

Conventional VLA systems rely heavily on data and trial-and-error learning. If a robot is asked to stack blocks into a tower, it must first analyze the scene, identify each block, and determine how to place them correctly.

This process often leads to mistakes. Shadows may confuse the system about a block's shape, or the robot may place pieces incorrectly, causing the structure to collapse.

These errors are similar to the problems seen in LLMs. Just as robots can misplace blocks, chatbots can generate false or misleading outputs. Examples include fabricating legal cases or producing images with unrealistic details such as extra fingers.

How Symbolic Reasoning Improves Accuracy and Efficiency

Symbolic reasoning offers a different strategy. Instead of relying only on patterns from data, it uses rules and abstract concepts such as shape and balance. This allows the system to plan more effectively and avoid unnecessary trial and error.

"Like an LLM, VLA models act on statistical results from large training sets of similar scenarios, but that can lead to errors," said Scheutz. "A neuro-symbolic VLA can apply rules that limit the amount of trial and error during learning and get to a solution much faster. Not only does it complete the task much faster, but the time spent on training the system is significantly reduced."

The researchers tested their system using the Tower of Hanoi puzzle, a classic problem that requires careful planning.

The neuro-symbolic VLA achieved a 95% success rate, compared with just 34% for standard systems. When given a more complex version of the puzzle that it had not encountered before, the hybrid system still succeeded 78% of the time. Traditional models failed every attempt.

Training time also dropped sharply. The new system learned the task in only 34 minutes, while conventional models required more than a day and a half.

Massive Energy Savings in Training and Use

Energy consumption was reduced dramatically as well. Training the neuro-symbolic model required only 1% of the energy used by a standard VLA system. During operation, it used just 5% of the energy needed by conventional approaches.

Scheutz compared this inefficiency to everyday AI tools. "These systems are just trying to predict the next word or action in a sequence, but that can be imperfect, and they can come up with inaccurate results or hallucinations. Their energy expense is often disproportionate to the task. For example, when you search on Google, the AI summary at the top of the page consumes up to 100 times more energy than the generation of the website listings."

The Growing Strain of AI on Power Infrastructure

As AI adoption accelerates across industries, demand for computing power continues to climb. Companies are building increasingly large data centers, some of which require hundreds of megawatts of electricity. That level of consumption can exceed the needs of entire small cities.

This trend has sparked a race to expand infrastructure, raising concerns about long-term energy limits.

The researchers suggest that current approaches based on LLMs and VLAs may not be sustainable in the long run. While these systems are powerful, they consume large amounts of energy and can still produce unreliable results.

In contrast, neuro-symbolic AI offers a different direction. By combining learning with structured reasoning, it may provide a more efficient and dependable foundation for future AI systems.

Materials provided by Tufts University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10784. 5 Best AI Anime Video Generators in 2026, and Are AI Anime Videos Worth Making?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI video generators can now turn a prompt or a character image into a few seconds of animation, and anime is one of the most popular styles people ask for. But anime is also one of the hardest styles to get right: flat colors, thin linework and stylized motion expose the weaknesses of video models quickly. For developers and designers, the useful question is not which tool is “best”, but what each one actually offers, what it cannot do yet, and how to fit it into a real production workflow.

This article compares five generators on what their own pages state, covers the problems they all share, asks whether AI anime videos are worth making, and then explains how the models work and how designers and developers can build a practical workflow, including ffmpeg and API-integration examples. “Best” here depends on the job: the tools are listed in no ranking order, each is linked once to its official site for reference, and the comparison is based on public vendor pages reviewed in November 2026. Features, models and pricing change often, so verify current details before relying on any of them.

We reviewed each vendor’s own site and compared what it states on the same points. This is a documentation-based comparison, not a lab benchmark. Where a page does not state something, we say so instead of guessing.

AI anime video generator by Pollo AI sits within a general AI video platform. On its main site, Pollo describes generating video from text prompts or uploaded images and editing existing videos, such as adding, changing, removing or restyling content. It also offers AI image generation, so a character image and the animation can be made in one place.

Strengths for anime work: text-to-video and image-to-video in the same tool, plus video editing and restyling, which suits experimenting with looks.

Limitations and gaps: the pages we could read do not name the underlying models, list credit amounts or pricing, or describe anime-specific controls such as reference characters or first and last frame inputs. You will need to test consistency yourself.

Fits: creators who want image, video and editing tools in one platform and are comfortable testing results before committing.

Vidu lists text-to-video, image-to-video and reference-to-video modes. For image-to-video you can set first and last frames. Reference-to-video lets you upload several images of a character, object or scene, and saved references can be reused through a My References feature. The site highlights 2D animation and diverse artistic styles, mentions 1080p output and offers an API platform aimed at enterprise use.

Strengths for anime work: the clearest set of consistency tools among the five pages we reviewed, with first and last frames, multi-image references and reusable saved characters.

Limitations and gaps: the page does not specify clip durations beyond examples, detail its anime-specific templates, or list prices. Free credits are mentioned without an amount.

Fits: sequences built around a recurring character, and teams that want an API.

Kling AI presents Kling 4.0 for video, described as offering stable dynamic motion and an audio-visual experience, alongside an image model, a Motion Control tool and a node-based Canvas editor for working through scripts, storyboards, images and video. It also lists MCP and CLI support for batch generation and automated production, and an API platform with documentation.

Strengths for anime work: motion-focused tooling and a workflow environment for storyboards and multi-step production, plus automation options for developers.

Limitations and gaps: the page we reviewed does not detail image-to-video specifics, first and last frame controls, camera control, durations, video resolution or pricing, and it gives no anime-specific guidance.

Fits: teams that value motion control and scripted or batch pipelines.

Hailuo AI, from MiniMax, describes creating AI videos and images from text or photos. Its page shows a reference field and an Omni Reference option, a banner for a model that mentions multimodal generation and editing, and many photo-based templates such as photo-to-motion, transformation and style transfer.

Strengths for anime work: an accessible, template-driven way to animate a still image and try stylized effects quickly.

Limitations and gaps: the page does not explain how reference works, or state camera control, durations, resolution, API availability or pricing. Template-driven results may be harder to control precisely than prompt- and frame-driven workflows.

Fits: quick experiments and social clips made from a single image.

Dreamina describes itself as an all-in-one creative platform for images, videos and talking avatars. For video it lists text-to-video, image-to-video, sketch-to-video, cartoon video generation and reference-to-video, using several Seedance models, along with editing examples such as green-screen replacement. It also offers image generation and upscaling.

Strengths for anime work: a broad toolset, including cartoon-oriented generation, references and editing in one environment.

Limitations and gaps: no official maximum duration or video resolution is stated, and the page notes that displayed pricing is for reference only and that final pricing requires signing in. The breadth of features and models can make it harder to know which one to use for a given shot.

Fits: creators who want images, video and avatars under one roof.

“Not stated” means the vendor page we reviewed did not say, not that the feature does not exist. Treat the table as a checklist of questions to answer in your own tests.

It depends on the job and on what you compare them with. Against a full studio production they are limited. Against having no animation at all, they change what a small team or solo creator can attempt.

Treat the generator as a fast prototyping and shot-making assistant, not a replacement for writing, direction and design. Audience reaction also varies, and some viewers are openly critical of AI-made work, so quality and disclosure both matter.

Most current generators are diffusion models extended to video. As in image diffusion, the model starts from random noise and removes it step by step, guided by your inputs. The extension to video, described in research such as Video Diffusion Models, is that the model denoises a whole sequence of frames together, so that motion and appearance stay coherent over time. Many systems work in a compressed latent space rather than on raw pixels to keep generation affordable.

Create an original character sheet with front, side and three-quarter views, a fixed color palette and a clear silhouette. Keep it simple: fewer fine details means less flicker.

Break the sequence into short shots with one action and one camera move each. A four-shot, twenty-second sequence is more realistic than one long generation.

Start each shot from a clean keyframe of your character. Where a tool supports first and last frames or reference images, use them to define the pose and keep identity stable.

Describe subject, action, camera and mood, and add a style anchor. For example: “The girl slowly turns toward the camera, wind moves her hair, petals drift past, gentle push-in, 2D cel shading, clean linework.”

Scrub through each clip for warped hands, drifting faces and flickering lines. Discard or regenerate failures instead of hoping nobody notices.

Join the best takes, color-match them, add sound and captions, and add disclosure where a platform requires it.

Developers can make the pipeline repeatable. Two patterns help: scripted post-processing with ffmpeg, and a resilient job-based client for any generation API.

The script below extracts a last frame to seed the next shot, normalizes clips to a common size and frame rate so they can be joined, concatenates them, and optionally holds each frame twice for an “on twos” look. Adjust sizes and frame rates to your project.

Video generation is usually asynchronous: you submit a job, then poll or receive a callback. The sketch below shows the pattern with a timeout and backoff. The endpoints and fields are placeholders, so follow your provider’s documentation for real ones.

Most are diffusion models for video. They start from noise and remove it step by step across a sequence of frames, guided by a text prompt and often a starting image or reference images, until coherent motion appears.

Usually, for consistency. The image locks the character and style, while the prompt only needs to describe motion.

Thin lines and flat color regions make small frame-to-frame changes visible. Simpler designs, slower motion and post-processing reduce it.

Partly. Reference images, saved characters and shared keyframes help, but you should still review every shot for drift.

For short clips, concept previews and storyboards, often yes. For long, dialogue-driven stories, not yet.

It depends on the tool and plan. Read the current terms and make sure you have rights to any characters or assets you upload.

Run the same character image and prompt through each, then compare consistency, line stability, motion quality and cost per usable second.

AI anime video generators are useful, but uneven. The five tools reviewed here differ in input modes, consistency tools, developer access and how much their own pages say, and none removes the core problems of consistency, flicker and limited acting. The practical approach is to test several tools on the same shots, keep your workflow tool-agnostic, design characters that are simple and consistent, and review every clip before it ships. Used that way, they are a capable prototyping and shot-making aid, not a substitute for design and direction.

#10782. This new chip survives 1300°F (700°C) and could change AI forever

Modern electronics power everything from smartphones to satellites, but they all share a major limitation. Heat. Once temperatures climb above roughly 200 degrees Celsius, most devices begin to break down. For decades, this thermal barrier has been one of the toughest challenges in engineering.

Researchers at the University of Southern California now believe they have found a way past that limit.

In a study published on March 26, 2026 in Science, a team led by Joshua Yang, Arthur B. Freeman Chair Professor at the Ming Hsieh Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the USC Viterbi School of Engineering and the USC School of Advanced Computing, unveiled a new type of memory device that continues to operate at 700 degrees Celsius (~1300 degrees Fahrenheit). That temperature exceeds molten lava and goes far beyond anything previously achieved for this class of technology. The device showed no sign of failure. In fact, 700 degrees was simply the maximum their equipment could test.

"You may call it a revolution," Yang said. "It is the best high-temperature memory ever demonstrated."

The new device is known as a memristor, a nanoscale component that can both store data and perform computations. It is constructed like a microscopic layered structure, with two electrodes on either side and a thin ceramic layer in between.

Jian Zhao, the study's first author, built the device using tungsten for the top electrode, hafnium oxide ceramic in the middle, and graphene for the bottom layer. Tungsten has the highest melting point of any element, while graphene, a single-atom-thick sheet of carbon, is known for its exceptional strength and heat resistance.

This combination produced remarkable performance. The device retained data for more than 50 hours at 700 degrees without needing to be refreshed. It also endured over one billion switching cycles at that temperature and operated at just 1.5 volts with speeds measured in tens of nanoseconds.

The discovery was not part of the team's original plan. They were initially attempting to create a different graphene-based device, which did not work as intended. Along the way, they encountered something surprising.

"To be honest, it was by accident, as most discoveries are," Yang said. "If you can predict it, it's usually not surprising, and probably not significant enough."

Further investigation revealed why the device performed so well. In conventional electronics, heat causes metal atoms in the top electrode to slowly migrate through the ceramic layer. Eventually, they reach the bottom electrode, creating a permanent connection that short-circuits the device and leaves it stuck in the on state.

Graphene prevents this failure. Its interaction with tungsten is, as Yang described it, similar to oil and water. Tungsten atoms that approach the graphene surface cannot attach to it. Without a stable point to settle, they drift away instead of forming a conductive bridge. This prevents short circuits and preserves the device's function even under extreme heat.

The researchers confirmed this mechanism using advanced electron microscopy, spectroscopy, and quantum-level simulations. By understanding what happens at the atomic interface, they have turned an unexpected result into a principle that could guide future designs. Other materials with similar surface properties could be identified, which may help scale the technology for industrial production.

Electronics capable of operating above 500 degrees Celsius have long been a goal for space exploration. Venus, for example, has a surface temperature around that level, and every lander sent there has failed in part due to extreme heat. Current silicon-based chips cannot survive such conditions.

"We are now above 700 degrees, and we suspect it will go higher," Yang said.

The potential applications go far beyond space missions. Geothermal energy systems require electronics that can function deep underground, where surrounding rock can glow red-hot. Nuclear and fusion systems also expose equipment to intense heat. Even in everyday settings, durability improves significantly. A device rated for 700 degrees would be extremely robust at the roughly 125-degree temperatures often reached inside automotive electronics.

Why It Matters for Artificial Intelligence

In addition to storing data, the device offers a major advantage for artificial intelligence. Many AI systems rely heavily on matrix multiplication, a mathematical operation used in tasks like image recognition and language processing. Traditional computers perform these calculations step by step, consuming large amounts of energy.

Memristors approach the problem differently. By using Ohm's Law, where voltage times conductance equals current, the device performs calculations directly as electricity flows through it. The result is obtained instantly as the measured current.

"Over 92 percent of the computing in AI systems like ChatGPT is nothing but matrix multiplication," Yang said. "This type of device can perform that in the most efficient way, orders of magnitude faster and at lower energy."

Yang and three co-authors of the study (Qiangfei Xia, Miao Hu, and Ning Ge) have already co-founded a company called TetraMem to commercialize memristor-based AI chips at room temperature. Their lab is already using working chips from TetraMem for machine learning tasks. The high-temperature version described in this research could extend those capabilities to environments where traditional electronics cannot operate, allowing devices such as spacecraft or industrial sensors to process data directly on site.

From Lab Prototype to Real-World Technology

Despite the promising results, Yang emphasizes that practical applications are still some distance away. Memory is only one part of a complete computing system. High-temperature logic circuits will also need to be developed and integrated. In addition, the current devices were built manually at very small scales in a laboratory setting, so manufacturing at scale will take time.

"This is the first step," Yang said. "It's still a long way to go. But logically, you can see: now it makes it possible. The missing component has been made."

From a manufacturing perspective, two of the materials used in the device, tungsten and hafnium oxide, are already widely used in semiconductor production. Graphene is newer but is actively being developed by major companies such as TSMC and Samsung, and it has already been produced at wafer scale in research environments.

The work was conducted through the CONCRETE Center, short for Center of Neuromorphic Computing under Extreme Environments, a multi-university Center of Excellence led by USC and supported by the Air Force Office of Scientific Research and the Air Force Research Laboratory. Key experimental work was carried out in collaboration with Dr. Sabyasachi Ganguli's team at the AFRL Materials Lab in Dayton, Ohio, while theoretical analysis involved USC researchers and collaborators at Kumamoto University in Japan.

For Yang, publication in Science reflects more than a single achievement.

"Space exploration has never been so real, so close, and at such a large scale," he said. "This paper represents a critical leap into a much larger, more exciting frontier."

Materials provided by University of Southern California. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10780. Ex-Rockstar artist explains main difference between Red Dead 2 and GTA 6 open world

A former Rockstar artist who worked on both GTA 6 and Red Dead Redemption 2 says Leonida’s countryside has slightly less detail than the natural world of Rockstar’s Western.

David O’Reilly made the comparison while exploring GTA 6’s Mount Kalaga region, an area he worked on before leaving Rockstar in 2023. He was impressed by what he found, describing the game’s presentation as “almost flawless,” but noticed a difference in where the two games put their detail.

O’Reilly believes Red Dead 2 gave its landscapes more attention because they made up so much of the game’s world, while GTA 6 has a dense modern city to bring to life alongside its countryside.

“I’m seeing a slight paring back in the natural world in GTA 6 from RDR2, but obviously a much greater emphasis and much more kind of detail in the urban environments,” O’Reilly said in a video sharing his first impressions.

He also found GTA 6’s cities more interactive and immersive, saying the difference reflected the kind of world each game was built to portray.

“GTA 6 is more a systems-driven modern urban world with countryside, natural landscapes, whereas RDR2 was almost purely natural landscapes,” he explained. This was especially noticeable in the game’s rivers, which he worked on as a water artist for Red Dead 2.

“Rivers were a bigger emphasis on RDR2. They were a big part of the world, and in this game they’re a lesser part of the world,” he said. After examining the water around Mount Kalaga, O’Reilly added that, “on balance,” he prefers Red Dead 2’s rivers and thinks GTA 6’s water is generally “a little bit too slow.”

He praised the way GTA 6’s water continues to react to players and objects even when they stop moving, though, calling it “a big plus point.” O’Reilly was also impressed by the riverbanks themselves, saying the rocks on either side were “blowing my mind.”

Despite giving Red Dead 2 the edge when it comes to natural landscapes, O’Reilly expects to spend plenty more time travelling around Leonida. After a canoe trip through Mount Kalaga, he said GTA 6 would probably replace Red Dead 2 as his “go-to chill game” when he wants to relax.

#10779. This new chip could slash data center energy waste

As data centers consume more energy to support growing digital demands, engineers at the University of California San Diego have introduced a new chip design that could make powering graphics processing units (GPUs) more efficient. The innovation focuses on a key function in electronics: converting high voltages into the lower levels required by computing hardware. In laboratory testing, a prototype chip successfully performed this type of voltage conversion with high efficiency under conditions similar to those found in modern data centers.

The findings, published in Nature Communications, suggest the potential for smaller and more energy-efficient systems in advanced computing environments.

Rethinking DC-DC Converters for Modern Electronics

At the center of the new design is an improved version of a widely used component known as a DC-DC step-down converter. These converters are found in nearly all electronic devices and serve as a critical link between power sources and sensitive circuits. Their job is to take a high incoming voltage and reduce it to the exact level needed for safe operation.

In data centers, electricity is often distributed at 48 volts, while GPU processors typically require much lower voltages, usually between 1 and 5 volts. Efficiently managing this large voltage drop has become increasingly challenging as computing systems grow more powerful and compact.

Limits of Traditional Power Conversion Technology

Conventional step-down converters often struggle when dealing with large differences between input and output voltage. As that gap increases, efficiency drops and it becomes harder to supply enough current. Most existing designs rely on magnetic components such as inductors. While these components have been refined over many years, they are approaching their practical limits and are becoming harder to improve further.

"We've gotten so good at designing inductive converters that there's not really much room left to improve them to meet future needs," said study senior author Patrick Mercier, professor in the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the UC San Diego Jacobs School of Engineering.

Exploring Piezoelectric Resonators as an Alternative

To move beyond these limitations, Mercier and his team, including first author Jae-Young Ko, an electrical and computer engineering Ph.D. student at UC San Diego, investigated a different approach using piezoelectric resonators. These small devices store and transfer energy through mechanical vibrations rather than magnetic fields.

Converters based on piezoelectric components could offer several advantages. They have the potential to be smaller, more energy dense, more efficient, and easier to manufacture at scale. "They have a lot of room to grow and have the potential to deliver better performance than anything that's come before them," Mercier said.

However, earlier versions of piezoelectric converters have had difficulty maintaining efficiency and delivering sufficient power when handling large voltage differences.

Hybrid Design Achieves High Efficiency and Power Output

To overcome these issues, the researchers created a hybrid converter that combines a piezoelectric resonator with small, commercially available capacitors arranged in a carefully designed configuration. This setup enables the system to handle larger voltage conversions more effectively.

The team incorporated this design into a prototype chip and tested its performance. The device successfully converted 48 volts down to 4.8 volts -- a level commonly required in data centers -- with a peak efficiency of 96.2 percent. It also delivered roughly four times more output current than previous piezoelectric-based designs.

This hybrid approach offers several benefits. It creates multiple pathways for energy to move through the system, reduces wasted power, and lessens the strain on the resonator. Together, these improvements enhance both efficiency and power delivery while only slightly increasing the chip's size.

Challenges and Next Steps for Real-World Use

Although the technology shows strong promise, it is still in the early stages of development. The researchers view it as an important step toward overcoming the constraints of current power conversion systems. Future efforts will focus on refining materials, improving circuit designs, and developing better packaging methods.

One challenge is that piezoelectric resonators physically vibrate, which means they cannot be attached to circuit boards using standard soldering techniques. New integration strategies will be needed to incorporate them into electronic systems, Mercier explained.

"Piezoelectric-based converters aren't quite ready to replace existing power converter technologies yet," Mercier added. "But they offer a trajectory for improvement. We need to continue to improve on multiple areas -- materials, circuits and packaging -- to make this technology ready for data center applications."

This project was supported in part by the Power Management Integration Center (PMIC), an Industry-University Cooperative Research Center (IUCRC) funded by the National Science Foundation (award number 2052809).

Materials provided by University of California - San Diego. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10777. “Giant superatoms” could finally solve quantum computing’s biggest problem

Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have introduced a new theoretical design for quantum systems based on what they call "giant superatoms." This concept offers a fresh way to protect, control, and share quantum information, potentially bringing scientists closer to building large-scale quantum computers.

Quantum computers are expected to transform fields like drug discovery and encryption by solving problems that are far beyond the reach of conventional machines. However, progress has been limited by a major challenge known as decoherence. This occurs when quantum bits, or qubits, lose their information due to interactions with their surroundings. Even small amounts of electromagnetic noise can disrupt the fragile quantum states needed for computation.

"Quantum systems are extraordinarily powerful but also extremely fragile. The key to making them useful is learning how to control their interaction with the surrounding environment," says Lei Du, postdoctoral researcher in applied quantum technology at Chalmers.

Lei Du is the lead author of a study that outlines this new type of quantum system. The design is built around giant superatoms, which combine several important features. These systems reduce decoherence, remain stable, and consist of multiple interconnected "atoms" that function together as a single unit.

Giant superatoms bring together two previously separate ideas in quantum physics: giant atoms and superatoms. While each has been studied on its own, this is the first time they have been merged into a single system. These structures behave like atoms but are not found in nature. Instead, they are engineered by scientists (see fact box below).

The idea of giant atoms was first introduced by researchers at Chalmers over a decade ago and is now widely used in the field. A giant atom is typically designed as a qubit (which is the smallest unit of quantum information). Unlike ordinary atoms, it connects to light or sound waves at multiple, physically separated points. This allows it to interact with its environment in several places at once, helping it preserve quantum information.

"Waves that leave one connection point can travel through the environment and return to affect the atom at another point -- similar to hearing an echo of your own voice before you've finished speaking. This self-interaction leads to highly beneficial quantum effects, reduces decoherence and gives the system a form of memory of past interactions," explains Anton Frisk Kockum, Associate Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Although giant atoms have improved understanding of quantum behavior, they have had limitations when it comes to entanglement. Entanglement allows multiple qubits to share a single quantum state and act as one coordinated system, which is essential for powerful quantum computers.

To overcome this limitation, the research team combined giant atoms with the concept of superatoms. A superatom consists of several natural atoms that share the same quantum state and behave collectively as one larger atom.

This combination is expected to make it easier to create complex quantum states needed for quantum communication, networks, and highly sensitive measurement systems.

"A giant superatom may be envisaged as multiple giant atoms working together as a single entity, exhibiting a non-local interaction between light and matter. This enables quantum information from multiple qubits to be stored and controlled within one unit, without the need for increasingly complex surrounding circuitry," explains Lei Du.

"Giant superatoms open the door to entirely new capabilities, giving us a powerful new toolbox. They allow us to control quantum information and create entanglement in ways that were previously extremely difficult, or even impossible," says Janine Splettstoesser, Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Toward Scalable and Practical Quantum Systems

This work creates new possibilities for building quantum systems that are both scalable and reliable. The researchers plan to move from theory toward actually constructing these systems. Their design could also be integrated with other quantum technologies, serving as a building block for connecting different types of quantum platforms.

"There is currently strong interest in hybrid approaches, in which different quantum systems work together, because each has its own strengths," says Anton Frisk Kockum. "Our research shows that smart design can reduce the need for increasingly complex hardware and giant superatoms are bringing us one step closer to practically applicable quantum technology."

More on: Methods for protecting, controlling and distributing quantum information

The study shows that the way giant superatoms interact with light depends on their internal quantum states. This discovery gives researchers greater control over how quantum information moves through a system. They describe two different ways of connecting these structures to achieve useful outcomes.

In one setup, several giant superatoms are closely linked in a specific arrangement. This allows them to pass quantum states between each other without decoherence, meaning no information is lost.

In another setup, the atoms are spaced farther apart but connected in a carefully tuned way so that waves remain synchronized. This makes it possible to direct quantum signals and distribute entanglement over long distances.

Superatoms and giant atoms are engineered systems that behave like atoms rather than naturally occurring ones.

A superatom is a quantum system made up of multiple natural atoms that share a single quantum state and respond to light as one entity.

A giant atom, on the other hand, connects to light or sound waves at several separate points in space. It is called "giant" because it is larger than the wavelength of the light it interacts with.

Giant atoms have defined energy levels and follow the rules of quantum mechanics, yet they can reach sizes of up to millimeters, making them visible to the naked eye. Through electromagnetic or acoustic waves, they can interact with their surroundings at multiple locations at the same time. One way to picture this is as a single atom linked to a wave at several distant points. This unusual setup allows the atom to be influenced by the waves it produces.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10773. This simple change stops robot swarms from getting stuck

Imagine a swarm of robots rushing to complete an urgent job, such as cleaning up an oil spill or assembling complex machinery. At first, adding more robots speeds things up. But after a certain point, the space becomes crowded, robots start interfering with one another, and overall progress slows.

This raises a simple but important question: in a limited area, how many robots can you deploy before efficiency starts to drop? Researchers at Harvard believe they have found a clear answer.

A new study from the lab of L. Mahadevan, the Lola England de Valpine Professor of Applied Mathematics, Organismic and Evolutionary Biology, and Physics, shows that adding a controlled amount of randomness to how robots move can reduce congestion and improve performance in crowded environments.

The work combines mathematical modeling, computer simulations, and real-world experiments. It demonstrates how basic local movement rules can lead to organized, efficient outcomes on a larger scale. The findings could influence how robotic fleets are designed and may even apply to human crowd management and traffic flow. The research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences and led by applied mathematics Ph.D. student Lucy Liu, with guidance from SEAS Senior Research Fellow Justin Werfel.

Why Randomness Helps Predict Complex Behavior

Studying dense crowds is difficult because individuals can take countless possible paths and interact in unpredictable ways, Liu explained. To simplify the problem, the researchers treated each robot as a basic unit with a small, adjustable amount of variation in its movement.

"This might be counterintuitive, because how could randomness make things easier to work with?" said Liu. "But in this case, when you have a lot of randomness, it becomes possible to take averages -- average distances, average times, average behaviors. This makes it a lot easier to make predictions."

To explore this idea, the team created computer simulations of robot groups, referred to as agents. Each agent started at a random location and was assigned a random destination. Once it reached its target, it immediately received a new one, mimicking continuous task assignment in real-world systems.

Each agent moved toward its goal with a tunable amount of variation, described as "noise." With no noise, agents moved in straight lines. With high noise, their paths became erratic and inefficient. However, this wandering also helped them navigate around one another.

The simulations revealed a clear pattern. When agents moved in perfectly straight paths, they quickly formed dense clusters and traffic jams that halted progress. When movement became too random, congestion disappeared but efficiency dropped due to excessive wandering.

Between these extremes, the researchers identified a sweet spot. In this range, agents occasionally bumped into one another and formed short-lived clusters, but still managed to slip past and keep moving. This balance allowed the system to maintain a steady flow.

Using these insights, the team developed formulas to estimate "goal attainment rate," or how many destinations are reached over time. These equations made it possible to determine the ideal combination of crowd density and movement randomness to maximize performance.

To confirm their findings, Liu collaborated with physicist Federico Toschi at Eindhoven University of Technology in the Netherlands. Together, they set up experiments with small wheeled robots in a lab equipped with an overhead camera.

Each robot carried a QR code so its position could be tracked and updated with new destinations. Although the physical robots moved more slowly and less precisely than the simulated agents, they displayed the same overall patterns.

The experiments supported a key idea: highly complex coordination does not require advanced intelligence or centralized control. Instead, simple local rules can produce effective group behavior, at least within certain density limits.

"Understanding how active matter, whether it is a swarm of ants, a herd of animals, or a group of robots, become functional and execute tasks in crowded environments using the principles of self-organization, is relevant to many questions in behavioral ecology," Mahadevan said. "Our study suggests strategies that might well be much broader than the instantiation we have focused on."

Liu noted that she has long been interested in designing safer and more efficient crowded spaces. This research points toward a future where the movement of large groups, whether robots, vehicles, or people, could be predicted and optimized using mathematical tools.

The results suggest that introducing controlled variability into movement patterns may improve flow in many real-world systems, from factory floors to city streets.

Funding for the research came from the National Science Foundation Graduate Research Fellowship Program under Grant No. DGE 2140743, along with grants from the Simons Foundation and the Henri Seydoux Fund.

Materials provided by Harvard John A. Paulson School of Engineering and Applied Sciences. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10769. Quantum AI just got shockingly good at predicting chaos

A new study led by researchers at UCL (University College London) shows that combining quantum computing with artificial intelligence can significantly improve predictions of complex physical systems over long periods. The hybrid approach outperforms leading models that rely only on conventional computers.

The results, published in Science Advances, could enhance simulations of how liquids and gases behave, known as fluid dynamics. These types of models are essential in fields such as climate science, transportation, medicine, and energy production.

The improved accuracy appears to come from how quantum computers process information. Unlike traditional computers that use bits set to either 1 or 0, quantum computers use qubits, which can exist as 1, 0, or anything in between. In addition, each qubit can influence others, allowing a relatively small number of qubits to represent an enormous number of possible states.

Professor Peter Coveney, senior author from UCL Chemistry and the Advanced Research Computing Centre, explained the challenge: "To make predictions about complex systems, we can either run a full simulation, which might take weeks -- often too long to be useful -- or we can use an AI model which is quicker but more unreliable over longer time scales.

"Our quantum-informed AI model means we could provide more accurate predictions quickly. Making predictions about fluid flow and turbulence is a fundamental science challenge but it also has many applications. Our method can be used in climate forecasting, in modeling blood flow and the interaction of molecules, or to better design wind farms so they generate more energy."

Although quantum computers are widely expected to surpass classical machines in power, their real-world use has so far been limited. This new approach integrates quantum computing into a specific stage of the AI training process.

Typically, AI models learn from large datasets generated by simulations or observations. In this case, the data is first processed by a quantum computer, which identifies key statistical patterns that remain stable over time. These patterns, known as invariant statistical properties, are then used to guide the training of an AI model running on a conventional supercomputer.

The quantum-informed AI system delivered about 20 percent greater accuracy compared to standard AI models that did not use quantum-derived patterns. It also maintained stable predictions over longer periods, even when modeling chaotic systems.

Another major advantage was efficiency. The method required hundreds of times less memory, making it far more practical for large-scale simulations.

This performance boost comes from two defining features of quantum computing. Entanglement allows qubits to influence each other regardless of distance, while superposition enables a qubit to exist in multiple states at once until measured. Together, these properties allow quantum systems to process vast amounts of information in a compact form.

Demonstrating Practical Quantum Advantage

First author Maida Wang of the UCL Centre for Computational Science said: "Our new method appears to demonstrate 'quantum advantage' in a practical way -- that is, the quantum computer outperforms what is possible through classical computing alone. These findings could inspire the development of novel classical approaches that achieve even higher accuracy, though they would likely lack the remarkable data compression and parameter efficiency offered by our method. The next steps are to scale up the method using larger datasets and to apply it to real-world situations which typically involve even more complexity. In addition, a provable theoretical framework will be proposed."

Co-first author Xiao Xue, from Advanced Research Computing at UCL, added: "In this work, we demonstrate for the first time that quantum computing can be meaningfully integrated with classical machine learning methods to tackle complex dynamical systems, including fluid mechanics. It is exciting to see this kind of 'quantum-informed' approach moving towards practical use."

The researchers suggest that quantum computers are particularly well suited to modeling these systems because they can compactly represent their underlying physics. Many complex systems behave in ways that resemble quantum effects, where changes in one region can influence distant parts of the system, similar to entanglement.

Overcoming Limits of Current Quantum Hardware

Current quantum computers face challenges such as noise, errors, and interference, which often require large numbers of measurements. The new method avoids these issues by using the quantum computer only once during the workflow, rather than repeatedly exchanging data between quantum and classical systems.

The study used a 20-qubit IQM quantum computer connected to powerful classical computing resources at the Leibniz Supercomputing Centre in Germany.

To function, quantum computers must operate at extremely low temperatures, around minus 273C (close to absolute zero, colder than anything in space).

The research was funded by UCL and the UK's Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council (EPSRC), with additional support from IQM Quantum Computers and the Leibniz Supercomputing Centre in Munich.

As researchers continue to scale up this approach, it could open the door to more accurate and efficient predictions across a wide range of scientific and engineering applications.

Materials provided by University College London. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
