#10674. Power BI MCP server: how to brief an AI agent to build accurate measures

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Power BI, Microsoft's reporting and dashboard tool, now lets an AI agent work on the data model behind a report. If you ask one to add a year-on-year revenue measure (a measure is Power BI's name for a saved calculation) with Microsoft's MCP server connected, it can write the formula and save it to the model without you opening the formula bar. When we tried this on a sample model, an agent inspected the model, wrote the measure, tested it and saved it in under four minutes.

The agent did a careful job, but its formula didn't follow the same rules the finance team uses to count revenue. Nobody had told it what "revenue" meant in this business, so it had to decide for itself. We then gave another agent a short brief that set out finance's rules for what counts as revenue and which date to count it by. The agent wrote the formula from those rules, and the result matched finance's figure exactly. The two results show how much difference it makes in how you brief an AI agent to build accurate measures.

We wanted to find out what an agent can see in a Power BI model and how to brief it so the measure matches the business's own definitions, with the setup arranged so that every change can be checked. Developers will know the principle from agents that write SQL or API code, where the brief ends up doing the job of a specification.

Because MCP (Model Context Protocol) is an open standard, the same server can be used from different AI clients, GitHub Copilot in VS Code and Claude among them, although some clients need extra sign-in setup for the hosted version.

Power BI has two current MCP servers, plus an older one, the Power BI Consumption MCP server, kept for existing integrations. The first of the current pair is the Power BI Authoring MCP server, which you may still see under the name of its package, powerbi-modeling-mcp. It lets an agent create and change the objects inside a semantic model, which is the layer of a Power BI report that holds the tables, the relationships between them and the calculations, kept separate from the charts on the page. It comes in two forms. The hosted version, still in preview, needs nothing installed. The local version is generally available and runs on your own machine, which makes it the one to use when the model is a file you hold yourself, whether that's open in Power BI Desktop, the free Windows app used to build reports, or saved as a project folder. Macs aren't supported by the local version, so on a Mac it has to be the hosted one, which works only with models published to Microsoft's cloud (a Fabric workspace).

The second is the Fabric IQ MCP server. Fabric IQ is part of Microsoft Fabric, the wider data platform that Power BI now sits within. It gives an agent read-only tools for finding published reports and models and running queries against them, within the permissions already set, so the agent can answer questions about the data but can't change a model.

Which one to use follows from the job. Anything that changes a model goes through the authoring server, whether that's a new measure, a relationship or a set of descriptions, and it has to be the local version if the model is a Desktop file or a project folder under source control. Fabric IQ is for agents that only need answers from models other people maintain.

An agent connected to a semantic model can see its metadata (table and column names, data types, relationships, existing measures and any descriptions someone has written) and can run queries against the data. Everything it produces is inferred from those clues. A model that has served a business for years carries names that made perfect sense to the people who built it, and a column called Amt on a table called Sales_v2 tells an agent far less than it tells them.

Names carry a lot of the meaning. A column called Amount could hold the value with VAT (the UK's sales tax) or without, before credit notes or after, and an agent asked for "revenue" has to pick one. If the model holds several revenue measures built for different reports, the agent has to choose between them, and the one it picks may have been built for a different purpose.

The formulas depend on context as well. Power BI measures use a syntax of their own called DAX, which stands for Data Analysis Expressions. A DAX result shifts with whatever the person viewing the report has filtered or clicked on, which is known as filter context. A developer might think of a measure as a function that reads implicit state set by its caller. The same measure can be right as a single total on a card and need different handling once it's split by product in a table or filtered by a date slicer (the on-page date filter), depending on how CALCULATE (the DAX function that changes which filters apply) is used. Which behaviour is correct depends on how the business wants the number to respond, and that's something the agent has to be told.

Relationships work the same way. Plenty of models have more than one date column on the sales table, say order date and dispatch date, with only one relationship active at a time. A DAX measure uses the active relationship unless it's written to switch to the other one, and the agent has no way of knowing which date your monthly reporting runs on unless something tells it.

In each case the agent can write a valid measure. Which of two valid measures matches the business's own definition is the part it can't see from the model.

To see this in practice, we built a small sales model in a Power BI Desktop file, working through the server itself, with invented data: 5,266 order lines, with order dates from January 2025 to October 2026. We gave the Sales table an Amt column holding each line's value after discount with 20% VAT added, next to LineTotal and Discount columns that excluded VAT, and an existing [Revenue] measure that summed Amt. There were two date columns, with the active relationship to the Date table on dispatch date and the order date relationship inactive. Orders stop at the end of October 2026, which made the current year a part year, a deliberate test. Nothing in the model had a description. In this made-up business, finance reports revenue net of VAT and discounts, by order date.

On 12 November 2026 we connected an AI agent (Anthropic's Claude Fable 5.2) to version 1.0.1 of the local server through a small Node.js script that speaks MCP over stdio. In all three runs the agent had the server's tools only, without Microsoft's powerbi-authoring plugin, which adds modelling guidance on top of them. It was also told that nobody was available to answer questions, so each measure was saved exactly as the agent wrote it, with no review in between. The first prompt was one sentence: "Add a year-on-year revenue measure."

Before writing anything, the agent listed the tables, relationships and columns, read the existing measure, sampled some rows and checked the date ranges. It worked out for itself that 2026 was a part year and limited the comparison to the same dates in 2025, which avoided a misleading result of -6.7%. From 15 sample rows it also spotted that Amt looked like the net figure plus 20%.

The measure it saved reused the existing [Revenue] measure and followed the active relationship. This is the core of it, with the date handling and blank checks left out. CurrentDates is defined in those lines as the dates up to the last order:

It returned growth of 21.5% for 2026, which is correct for revenue counted by dispatch date. On finance's definition, by order date, the answer is 18.6%, and the whole difference came from that choice of date. The VAT question made no difference to a percentage, because Amt was already net of discount and every line carried the same rate, although it would have overstated any figure in pounds by 20%.

We reset the model and repeated the run with a fresh agent, which made the same two choices and returned the same 21.5%. Both choices were reasonable readings of the model as it stood, and the DAX was sound. In their reports, both agents flagged the date basis and the VAT treatment as open questions, and both worked out that an order-date basis would give 18.6%. That's what a good analyst would do, and with those two answers they would have had everything they needed.

Think of a capable analyst on their first morning, someone who knows DAX well but has never seen your business before and knows nothing (or not a lot) about it. You wouldn't say "add a revenue measure" and walk off. You'd tell them which table holds the trusted figures, whether revenue includes VAT, which date drives the reporting calendar, and what they shouldn't touch. An agent needs that briefing for the same reasons, and getting good work out of it draws on the skills you'd use with any new starter. Developers do the same thing when they write a good ticket. A request for a measure is a requirement, and it needs the same definitions and constraints, plus a way to check the result.

Before you send a prompt, ask yourself whether you could do the task properly if you were given only the words in that prompt and the model itself. If the answer is no, then the agent is in the same position, and it will make its own assumptions.

For the detailed run we cleared the earlier measure from the model and gave a fresh agent this brief:

This time the measure summed LineTotal minus Discount and used USERELATIONSHIP, the DAX function that switches a calculation to an inactive relationship, to work from order date. It returned 18.6% from that one brief, with no follow-up prompt, which is the figure we had calculated separately from the source data. This is the full measure as the agent saved it:

Each line of the brief answered a question the agent would otherwise have had to settle alone. It also made some sensible decisions the brief hadn't covered. It left the grand total blank, for instance, where a plain total across both years would have shown a meaningless growth figure of 151.2%. The final line of the brief, asking to see the DAX before anything is saved, is there so that a person can see choices like that. In the test we approved that step in advance, to keep the run unattended.

One detail needs adjusting before this measure is reused on other data. Its guard checks that each comparison period starts on or after the first order date, which works here because the sample data has an order on 1 January 2025. When we swapped that date for 2 January 2025 in a copy of the measure, January 2026 and the 2026 row came back blank. Comparing against the first date in the Date table, as the first agent's measure did, brought the 2026 figure back, since the Date table starts on 1 January. That version doesn't confirm that sales exist for the whole comparison period, though, so the right check depends on whether the business wants growth shown only where a complete prior period exists. That's a decision for whoever reviews the measure.

We ran the one-sentence prompt twice and the detailed brief once, all with the same AI model and one small set of data, which is enough to illustrate the pattern but too little to say how often it happens.

Good briefs help, but you'll be writing the same definitions into prompts again and again unless they live in the model itself. Descriptions on tables, columns and measures are part of the metadata the agent reads, so filling them in is one of the cheapest ways to put business definitions in front of it every time. In our test, where nothing had a description, both agents given the one-sentence prompt noted that nothing confirmed what Amt contained. A description such as "Net sales value in GBP, excluding VAT, after credit notes" stays in the model, where the agent reads it without anyone retyping it.

Naming deserves the same attention. Spelling out abbreviated column names, hiding technical keys that no report needs, gathering measures into a single measures table, and removing measures that are no longer used all give the agent fewer things to choose between. These are the same habits that make a model easier for a new colleague to pick up, so asking someone outside the team to find a figure in it is a fair test of the result.

Microsoft has also added a set of Prep data for AI features (in preview) to Power BI Desktop and the service. They let you choose which fields Copilot, Microsoft's AI assistant, should consider and write AI instructions that explain your business terms, with verified answers available for common questions. They're aimed at Copilot, but deciding and writing down your definitions is the same work whichever AI tool reads them.

An agent working through the authoring server has the same access as the person signed in, so anything that person can change, the agent can change. Sign in with an account that has no more access than the job needs, and take a copy of the model first, as you would before any bulk edit.

Start with read-only mode. The local server takes a --readonly flag that blocks all write operations, which is the right setting while you're learning how the agent behaves on your models. When we tried to create a measure in this mode, the server refused and said it was running read-only. In VS Code, the configuration looks like this (other clients use a similar structure, sometimes under an mcpServers key instead of servers):

You'll need Node.js installed for the npx command. The server won't run other tools until its licence terms (the EULA) have been accepted. Once you've read them, you can either tell the agent to accept for you or add --accepteula to the args list.

The --readonly flag belongs to the local server. On a Mac the route is the hosted server and a copy of the model in a Fabric workspace, once a Fabric administrator has switched on the tenant setting that allows the MCP endpoint, and there the copy is the safeguard. For an enforced limit, sign in with an account that holds only Build permission on the model, which lets the agent read and query it and nothing more.

Read-write is the default, so check how confirmation behaves in the version you install. Version 1.0.1 skips confirmation prompts by default, which the project's README hasn't caught up with, and has a --require-confirmation flag to turn them on. The flag is listed in the server's own --help output. In our runs every edit went through without a prompt. When we added the flag to the args list, the server asked for confirmation before the first write, with a message beginning "Are you sure you want to perform operations that will modify your database", and answering no blocked the change.

Version control is the other safeguard. Saved in the Power BI Project format (PBIP), a report and its model become folders of text files instead of one binary .pbix file. Commit those before the agent starts and its work arrives as an ordinary diff, which you can review and revert like any other change.

Whatever the agent reads, including table and column names and the rows returned by any query, is passed on to the AI provider behind your client, which may sit outside Microsoft's compliance commitments. For a model holding customer or payroll data, check that the provider is approved under your data policies before you connect.

An agent can draft and test a measure in minutes, and someone still has to read what comes back. For a developer new to Power BI, that takes enough DAX to see what a measure's filters will do in a table broken down by month or product, and enough knowledge of the business to confirm the agent used the right column and the right date. Writing the brief takes the same knowledge. Ours could only have come from someone who knew the model had an inactive relationship and which columns carried VAT.

These are data modelling and DAX skills, which many learners find the hardest part of Power BI. If the person reviewing agent output hasn't had that grounding yet, it helps to know how to judge whether a Power BI course covers enough DAX and data modelling, because courses vary a great deal in how much time they give to those two subjects compared with building charts.

Take a copy of a model you know well and connect the local server to it in read-only mode. Ask the agent to describe every table and measure in its own words, then check those descriptions against what you know the model does. Wherever the two differ, the model needs a clearer name or a written description, and the corrected text can go straight into the description fields. With those in place before you allow any writes, the agent starts its first real task with the same definitions your finance team uses.

#10673. GTA 6 players are devasted by train ‘nerf’ after spending years trying to stop it in GTA 5

GTA 6 players have found how to stop the trains circulating Leonida, and it’s actually a lot easier than they expected it to be.

In GTA 5, players spent years trying to bring the train to a halt. They tried everything from blasting it with explosives to parking huge vehicles and planes in its way.

However, no matter how hard they tried, it would always keep moving forward, laying waste to whoever or whatever was trying to block its path.

Now that they’ve been let loose in GTA 6, many tried to do the same once again, but they quickly found it’s nowhere near the challenge it once was.

In GTA 6, trains behave much more realistically. If you stand on the tracks or block them with a vehicle and give the driver enough time to spot you, they will bring the locomotive to a halt until you move out of the way.

You can Finally Derail a Moving Train in GTA 6 pic.twitter.com/PQItm03f7e

As you might expect, others have found more dramatic ways to slow it down, such as crashing a dump truck into the front of them. In fact, if you hit one with enough force at the right angle, they can be derailed completely.

Given how much GTA 6 steps up the realism, it’s probably no surprise that its trains aren’t unstoppable forces of nature. However, while some fans like this approach, others miss the challenge of trying to get a train to stop.

They nerferd the boss pic.twitter.com/jK8EihWCX6

“The nerfed the boss,” wrote one player on X, before adding: “I like the aura farming of GTA 5 train more.”

“THE TRAIN LOST IT’S ENTIRE AURA IN GTA 6,” wrote another.

#10672. A “quantum bath” puts quantum entanglement on autopilot

Future quantum computers may depend on the ability to connect widely separated modules through distributed entanglement. Until now, creating this kind of connection has generally required active control and repeated measurements. Physicists at the Institute of Science and Technology Austria (ISTA) have now demonstrated a fully autonomous alternative based on a "quantum bath" made from correlated particles of light.

Published in Physical Review X, the experiment provides the first demonstration of a prediction proposed more than 20 years ago. The approach could eventually offer a new foundation for practical quantum technologies.

Entanglement is one of the defining phenomena of quantum physics. It allows particles or systems to share correlations that cannot be explained by classical physics. Creating distributed entanglement between physically separated qubits (quantum bits) could be important for building larger quantum computers and future quantum networks.

Previous attempts to entangle distant qubits have generally followed one of two strategies. One method sends a single, actively controlled photon from one qubit to another. Another has each qubit emit a photon, with the two photons then matched in an effort to generate entanglement.

The second approach was recognized by the 2022 Nobel Prize in Physics. However, it still depends on repeated measurements and post-selection, and even then, the process does not always successfully produce entanglement.

PhD student Alejandro Andrés-Juanes and professor Johannes Fink at the Institute of Science and Technology Austria (ISTA) worked with international collaborators to develop a different solution. Their system uses a quantum bath that automatically brings distant qubits into synchronization. In a prototype device, the researchers used a shared source of correlated light particles to entangle two separated qubits, experimentally realizing an idea that had remained theoretical for more than two decades.

Quantum entanglement can take several forms. Continuous-variable entangled states can be produced efficiently and are therefore relatively accessible. They can be compared with a pendulum, whose position and momentum change continuously.

Many useful quantum technologies, however, depend on "discrete-variable" systems. These involve 'all-or-nothing' forms of entanglement that stationary qubits can use. The challenge for the ISTA team was finding a way to connect these readily available continuous forms of entanglement with the discrete forms needed for practical applications.

"In this work, we aimed to overcome this mismatch between the readily available and the practically useful forms of entanglement," says Andrés-Juanes. "By stabilizing the entangled states remotely, our approach is fully autonomous and requires no active control or measurement."

A Quantum Bath Powered by Correlated Light

Maintaining both entanglement and quantum coherence is one of the major difficulties facing quantum computing.

The researchers addressed that problem by making the qubits' surrounding environment itself responsible for producing and stabilizing entanglement.

"In our method, the quantum bath -- meaning the qubits' environment -- is the source of entanglement. It creates a new ground state through a continuous stream of correlated photons," says Fink. "This way, the entangled qubit state is stabilized, even beyond the qubits' own 'lifetime', and remains always available as a resource for further quantum processing. This makes the approach conceptually significant."

Because the entangled state remains available, researchers can access it whenever it is needed. That differs from temporary entanglement, which has to be used during the brief period when it exists.

To couple the qubits with the entangled photon source, the researchers relied on microwave photons. These low-energy light particles are especially useful for manipulating quantum information and are already central to leading superconducting-qubit technology.

Optical photons serve a different role and are commonly used in optics and atomic physics. They may also become important for carrying quantum information between distant quantum computers through fiber optics, an area that the Fink group at ISTA is also investigating.

The researchers needed to confirm that the two qubits were actually synchronized inside the quantum bath. To do that, they used quantum tomography, a technique that reconstructs a quantum system by examining many different 'slices' of its behavior.

"Qubits can be in a superposition of states, but all these states collapse when we measure them, leaving us with a 0 or 1 state," says Andrés-Juanes.

Quantum tomography allowed the researchers to perform measurements lasting only 20-80 nanoseconds and use those observations to investigate the qubits' underlying states. A nanosecond is one billionth of a second.

A 20-Year Prediction Becomes an Experiment

By successfully entangling two isolated qubits through a quantum bath, the ISTA researchers created a proof-of-concept laboratory prototype for the long-standing theoretical proposal.

"We present a relatively simple method that could be scaled up to synchronize multiple distant qubits," says Andrés-Juanes.

The new approach is promising, but it is not yet as efficient as methods that actively control qubit states. "Our method currently transfers about 10% of the bath's available entanglement."

The researchers suggest that one reason the idea took more than two decades to demonstrate is that the original theory was developed under idealized conditions that are difficult to reproduce experimentally.

"Our experiments helped us reveal several factors that may have prevented scientists from designing a functional quantum bath using a single source of correlated photons for distributed entanglement," says Fink.

The prototype developed at ISTA could provide new opportunities for quantum-optics experiments. It may also contribute to efforts to expand quantum processors and ultimately move them closer to fault-tolerant operation.

Materials provided by Institute of Science and Technology Austria. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10668. Scientists just made quantum computer operations 1,000 times faster

Quantum computers remain highly vulnerable to errors and tiny disturbances from their surroundings. The longer a quantum operation takes to complete, the more time there is for those errors to build up. Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have now developed a method that can perform a broad range of advanced quantum operations more than a thousand times faster. The advance tackles a major obstacle in the field and could help move quantum computing closer to becoming fault-tolerant.

Quantum computers could eventually transform areas such as drug discovery, energy technology, cryptography, artificial intelligence, and logistics. Before that can happen, however, these machines need to become much more dependable.

A major challenge is that quantum computations can be disrupted by extremely small environmental effects. Electrical noise, cosmic radiation, and overheating can all introduce errors while information is being processed.

Traditional computers can experience errors too, but decades of development have produced reliable error correction methods that can quickly detect and repair them. Quantum systems are much harder to protect because the information they use is extraordinarily delicate.

"The fundamental building blocks of quantum computers, known as qubits, are so sensitive that even the smallest disturbance can cause the quantum state to deviate from the target, resulting in the loss of information. If too many errors accumulate before they can be corrected, the computation can fail," says Lei Du, researcher in Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden, and lead author of the theoretical study published in the journal Physical Review Letters.

A Different Way to Protect Quantum Information

To make quantum computing more resilient and eventually fault-tolerant, researchers are investigating new ways to shield quantum information from errors. One promising strategy uses so-called bosonic quantum codes*.

Instead of assigning quantum information to individual qubits, this approach stores it in microwave fields inside superconducting circuits.

"Rather than storing quantum information in individual qubits, bosonic codes encode information in the microwave fields found within superconducting circuits. This approach has been shown to provide stronger protection against certain types of errors," explains Tangyou Huang, researcher in Quantum Technology at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Quantum Operations More Than 1,000 Times Faster

Working with bosonic quantum codes is not simple. Creating and controlling the required quantum states has traditionally involved guiding a quantum system through thousands of repeated driving cycles.

That process can take considerable time, and every additional cycle creates another opportunity for outside disturbances to interfere with the calculation. In quantum computing, speed is therefore closely tied to reliability.

Chalmers researchers Lei Du and Tangyou Huang have now proposed a different strategy. Rather than constructing the desired quantum states one small piece at a time, their method can perform a wide variety of operations much faster.

"Our method shows that a diverse range of quantum operations on bosonic states can be completed within a single driving cycle, rather than the several thousand cycles that have been required previously. This makes the operations both faster and more efficient, while reducing the risk that disturbances will corrupt the information before the process is finished. It represents an important step towards fault-tolerant quantum computers," says Lei Du.

The new approach is built around Quantum lattice gates, a recently proposed universal set of quantum gates developed by the same research team.

These gates act somewhat like shortcuts. Instead of requiring a long sequence of repeated control steps, they can allow the intended quantum operation to be completed in just one driving cycle. That could make the process faster, simpler, and less vulnerable to errors.

"You can think of it like building a large Lego castle. Instead of assembling it brick by brick and risking mistakes along the way, quantum lattice gates act like pre-built Lego modules that can be connected quickly and efficiently," says Tangyou Huang.

Designed for Superconducting Quantum Computers

The technique is especially well suited to superconducting quantum computers, which are among the most prominent technologies being developed in the international push toward large-scale quantum computing.

Chalmers University of Technology is also using superconducting technology as it develops a 100-qubit quantum computer.

"A key advantage of our approach is that it can be implemented using existing superconducting quantum circuit platforms. We are already discussing possible experimental realizations with colleagues at Chalmers, and we hope to see a demonstration of the method in the near future," says Tangyou Huang.

The researchers say the work addresses a central problem facing the field: efficiently producing and controlling quantum states that are capable of helping correct errors.

"Our results address one of the major bottlenecks in the field: how to quickly and reliably create and control the error-correcting quantum states that could play an important role in future quantum computers," says Lei Du.

More About Bosonic Codes, Quantum Lattice Gates and Floquet Control

*Bosonic quantum codes store quantum information in the states of, for example, microwave or optical resonators rather than in individual qubits. They are considered a promising tool for quantum error correction because they can provide built-in protection against certain types of errors.

Quantum operations are needed to process that stored information. Quantum lattice gates are a recently proposed collection of basic building blocks that can be used to control bosonic quantum states. By combining and designing these gates, researchers can carry out a wide range of more complicated quantum operations.

One method for implementing these operations is known as Floquet control, which uses periodic control signals to drive a quantum system. Earlier Floquet-based techniques have generally depended on slower processes involving many repeated driving cycles. The approach developed at Chalmers can instead perform quantum lattice gates directly in a single driving cycle, making some operations more than a thousand times faster.

The scientific paper "Single-Period Floquet Control of Bosonic Codes with Quantum Lattice Gates" has been published in Physical Review Letters. The authors are Tangyou Huang, Lei Du, and Lingzhen Guo. The researchers are affiliated with Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden and Tianjin University in China.

The research was funded by the National Natural Science Foundation of China (NSFC), the Wallenberg Centre for Quantum Technology (WACQT), and the Knut and Alice Wallenberg Foundation.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10665. Tiny nanolaser could cut computer energy use in half

The development of a nanolaser could mark an early step toward a future in which microchips communicate entirely with particles of light.

Researchers at DTU have created a tiny nanolaser that could eventually help make computers, smartphones, and data centers faster while using much less energy. The breakthrough was published in the scientific journalScience Advances.

In the future, thousands of these lasers could potentially be integrated onto a single microchip. Instead of moving information around a chip with electrical signals, devices could transmit data using photons, the particles that make up light.

"The nanolaser opens up the possibility of creating a new generation of components that combine high performance with minimal size. This could be in information technology, for example, where ultra-small and energy-efficient lasers can reduce energy consumption in computers, or in the development of sensors for the healthcare sector, where the nanolaser's extreme light concentration can deliver high-resolution images and ultrasensitive biosensors," says DTU professor Jesper Mørk.

Mørk co-authored the study with, among others, Drs. Meng Xiong and Yi Yu from DTU Electro.

Much of the internet already relies on light to carry information through fiber optic cables. Inside computers, however, data still moves through electronic circuits using electricity. That approach produces heat and can limit how quickly information can be transferred.

Nanolasers could bring optical communication directly onto microchips. By generating light signals efficiently inside the chip, they could allow information to move with very little energy loss, potentially making future devices faster, cooler, and more energy efficient.

Mørk estimates that using nanolasers in computers could reduce energy consumption by as much as half.

The compact DTU nanolaser is an important step toward that goal. Future chips designed around light-based communication would likely require thousands of extremely small, efficient lasers working together to transmit information across the chip.

The new nanolaser was created in DTU's clean room facility, DTU Nanolab. According to Mørk, the device pushes beyond the conventional limit for how small a laser can be made.

At its core is a structure known as a nanocavity, which traps and concentrates light within an exceptionally tiny space. Until now, achieving such intense confinement at this scale had been considered extremely difficult.

When researchers shine a beam of light onto the device, both photons and electrons become concentrated in the same microscopic region. This interaction allows the laser to function at room temperature while requiring unusually little energy.

The light-trapping structure used in the nanolaser was originally developed by Professor Ole Sigmund's group at DTU Construct.

The next major challenge is making the nanolaser operate using electrical power. If researchers can achieve that, the technology could have broad applications across computing, communications, and healthcare.

Computers and smartphones could potentially deliver greater performance while consuming less electricity. Data centers, which require enormous amounts of power, could also see substantial reductions in energy use, potentially leading to significant climate benefits.

In healthcare technology, the nanolaser's ability to concentrate light into an extremely small area could support ultra-sensitive sensors and high-resolution imaging systems.

Researchers estimate that the remaining technical challenges could be solved within the next 5-10 years.

Materials provided by Technical University of Denmark. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10663. Show, Don't Certify: How to Prepare for Your First Data Analyst Job

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Getting shortlisted for a junior data analyst job depends less on how many courses you have finished and more on what you can show. This plan breaks preparation into eight skill areas, pairs each one with a learning option and a hands-on task, and gives you a simple test for knowing when to move on.

Many people preparing for their first analyst role study for months and still struggle to get interviews. Their CVs list SQL, Excel and Tableau, yet nothing on the page shows those tools being used to answer a real question. Recruiters reading dozens of similar applications need something more concrete.

A course can give your learning a sensible order, but a completion badge says little about how you think. A small analysis that states its question, explains its method and admits its limits tells a hiring manager far more. This plan aims to collect that kind of proof while you learn, not after.

Start by collecting ten to twenty entry-level postings in the industry you want to join, and tally the tools and tasks they mention. You will quickly see a pattern. Some roles are built around Excel and routine reporting, while others want SQL, a BI platform and a bit of Python.

The wider market follows a similar shape. Jobright’s 2026 analysis of data analyst hiring, which looked at 2025–2026 postings from LinkedIn, Indeed and Levels.fyi, found that more than 80% of data analyst jobs mention SQL, over 60% mention Excel or Google Sheets, and roughly half mention Python or R. Let your own tally decide the order you learn in, but expect SQL to sit near the top.

As you work through each area below, finish with something you can show someone: a query, a tidied dataset, a chart, a one-page report or a portfolio project. Free material is often enough if you can stay disciplined. Pay for teaching only when it gives you something you cannot get alone, such as feedback, accountability or a fixed timetable.

SQL is how analysts pull, join and summarise records from a database, and a tool like Tableau lets other people explore what you found. Treat them as one workflow rather than two subjects: frame a question, write the query, sanity-check the numbers and present the result so a non-technical reader can follow it.

SQLBolt offers free, bite-sized SQL lessons that run in the browser, and Tableau publishes free training videos on connecting to data and building dashboards. Used together, they are enough to produce a small end-to-end project.

If you learn better with a set schedule and an instructor checking your work, you could learn to analyse data with SQL and Tableau from scratch through a guided programme at Heicoders Academy in Singapore. Whichever route you take, compare what is covered, how feedback is given, when sessions run and the full cost against what free resources already offer.

Proof you can show: A working SQL query set and your first published dashboard.

Move on when: You can take a question from idea to dashboard on your own, without following a tutorial line by line.

Spreadsheets are still where much of a team’s day-to-day reporting lives, especially when colleagues don’t write SQL. Get comfortable with filtering, pivot tables, and lookup functions, and learn to spot quiet errors like dates saved as text or one category spelled three different ways.

The next step is automating the cleanup so it doesn’t have to be repeated by hand each month. Microsoft’s Power Query documentation shows how to import and reshape data in recorded steps that refresh when new data arrives. The same ideas apply in other spreadsheet tools too.

Proof you can show: A messy export turned into a summary that refreshes itself.

Move on when: You can clean an untidy CSV and build a report that updates when the source file changes.

For most junior analyst roles, the technical interview is a SQL test. Basic SELECT statements won’t be enough; interviewers like to probe joins, subqueries, common table expressions and window functions, which answer everyday questions such as running totals or each customer’s first purchase.

Practise with timed problem sets and review your answers for correctness as well as speed.

Proof you can show: Confidence going into a live or take-home SQL test.

Move on when: You can solve intermediate problems in around 15 to 20 minutes each without looking up syntax.

Take-home assignments often come with data that has been made messy on purpose. Reviewers want to see how you deal with missing values, duplicates and inconsistent formats, and whether they can follow your reasoning afterwards.

Keep a short cleaning log as you work, noting each change and why you made it. A separate sheet tab or a list of comments in a notebook both work.

Proof you can show: A take-home submission a reviewer can audit step by step.

Move on when: Every change to your dataset is written down with a reason.

Case-study questions test whether you can read numbers sensibly, not just produce them. A working grasp of distributions, means and medians, spread, correlation and sampling will keep you away from the mistakes that undo otherwise good answers, such as quoting an average that a few extreme values have pulled out of shape.

Proof you can show: Clear, careful answers in case-study interviews.

Move on when: You can explain in everyday language why two things moving together doesn’t mean one causes the other.

Every chart should help someone answer a specific question. Learn where bars, lines, scatter plots and simple tables each work best, and how axis ranges, labels and colour can nudge a reader towards the wrong conclusion. On a dashboard, the most important finding should be the first thing people see.

Datawrapper’s guidance on picking chart types is a useful free reference. As practice, show the same result in two different chart types and decide which one represents the data more honestly.

Proof you can show: Portfolio charts that make a point, not just show off features.

Move on when: A person who has never seen your data can spot the main finding and its caveat at a glance.

What counts as a useful metric depends on the role. Marketing analysts focus on conversion and retention, operations analysts on delivery times and capacity, and finance analysts on margins and cash flow. Choose one sector and learn exactly how its key measures are defined.

Data.gov hosts a large, searchable collection of public datasets you can practise on for free. Before drawing any conclusions, read each dataset’s documentation, check when it was last updated and confirm its terms of use.

Proof you can show: A project built around the questions your target industry actually asks.

Move on when: You can define several sector metrics and describe how a manager would use each one.

Your portfolio is where the other seven areas come together. Two or three deep, well-documented projects will say more about you than a long list of quick ones. Give each project a README that sets out the question, the data, your method, the findings and the limitations.

Proof you can show: A portfolio link and a write-up you can talk through in an interview.

Move on when: Every project clearly explains what it asked, how you answered it and what you found.

There is no need to finish all eight areas before you apply. Once you can use the tools your target roles mention, speak honestly about your work and point to two or three clearly explained projects, start sending applications and keep learning alongside them.

Adjust each application to the job. Lead with a reporting example for spreadsheet-heavy roles, a query that solved a real problem for SQL roles, and a chart that supported a decision for visualisation roles. Be upfront about which work you built independently and which came from guided exercises.

Do I really need eight courses? No. These are skill areas, not eight required qualifications. A single resource can cover several of them, and practice you do on your own counts as much as classroom learning.

Is it possible to prepare using only free material? Yes. Free tutorials, open textbooks, official documentation and public datasets cover the fundamentals well. The harder parts are sticking to a routine and finding people who will give you honest feedback.

Which should come first, SQL or spreadsheets? It depends on the jobs you want. Spreadsheets are an easy starting point for reporting roles, while SQL is expected wherever databases are involved. You will want both eventually, so start with whichever your target postings stress most.

How many portfolio projects should I have? Two or three solid ones are a good start. Each should show a clear question, steps someone else could repeat and an honest reading of the results, rather than several near-identical dashboards.

Do I need a certificate before I apply? Not usually. Some employers ask for one, but many judge skills through interviews, take-home tasks and tests instead. Go by what each posting actually requires.

#10661. AI uncovers hidden Ozempic side effects across 400,000 Reddit posts

Artificial intelligence is giving researchers a new way to listen to what patients are saying about popular GLP-1 drugs. After analyzing more than 400,000 Reddit posts, a University of Pennsylvania team identified several symptoms reported by people using semaglutide (Ozempic, Wegovy, and Rybelsus) and tirzepatide (Mounjaro and Zepbound) that may not be fully represented in clinical trials or official regulatory information.

The study, published recently in Nature Health, examined more than five years of posts from nearly 70,000 Reddit users. Two categories stood out as particularly deserving of further investigation: reproductive symptoms, including changes in menstrual cycles, and problems involving body temperature, such as chills and hot flashes.

The findings do not establish that the medications caused these symptoms. Instead, researchers say the massive collection of spontaneous patient reports may reveal signals worth examining more closely.

"Some of the side effects we found, like nausea, are well known, and that shows that the method is picking up a real signal," says Sharath Chandra Guntuku, Research Associate Professor in Computer and Information Science (CIS) at Penn Engineering and the study's senior author. "The underreported symptoms are leads that came from patients themselves, unprompted, and clinicians could potentially pay attention to them."

What Patients Report Outside Clinical Trials

Clinical trials are designed to determine whether treatments work and to identify important safety problems, but they cannot necessarily capture every symptom that matters to patients once a medication is being used by a much larger population.

"Clinical trials generally identify the most dangerous side effects of drugs," adds Lyle Ungar, Professor in CIS and a co-author on the study. "But they can fail to find what symptoms patients are most concerned about; even though social media is not necessarily representative, a large collection of posts may reflect additional concerns."

The distinction is important. The study found associations in what people discussed online, not proof that GLP-1 drugs were responsible for those experiences.

"We can't say that GLP-1s are actually causing these symptoms," notes Neil Sehgal, the study's first author and a doctoral student in CIS advised by Guntuku and Ungar. "But nearly 4% of the Reddit users in our sample reported menstrual irregularities, which would be even higher in a female-only sample. We think that's a signal worth investigating."

Using Social Media as an Early Health Signal

The idea of mining online conversations for clues about drug safety predates today's AI boom. In 2011, Ungar participated in one of the earliest efforts to use material created by internet users to identify possible adverse effects from medications.

Social media can capture experiences that patients discuss with one another but may never formally report to a doctor, drug manufacturer, or regulator.

"Online patient communities work a lot like a neighborhood grapevine," says Ungar. "People who are living with these medications are swapping notes with each other in real time, sharing experiences that rarely make it into a doctor's office visit or an official report."

Since then, online patient communities have expanded enormously. That has made social media a potentially valuable source for studying how medications affect people in everyday life, although gaining access to platform data has become more difficult.

Traditional clinical research remains essential, the researchers emphasize, but online conversations can provide information far more quickly when millions of people begin using a drug.

"Clinical trials are the gold standard, but by design, they are slow," says Guntuku. "This is not a replacement for trials, but it can move much faster, and that speed matters when a drug goes from niche to mainstream almost overnight."

AI Makes Massive Social Media Analysis Possible

One of the biggest obstacles has always been scale.

Guntuku describes the approach as "computational social listening," which uses computational methods to identify patterns in large collections of online conversations about health.

Patients, however, rarely describe symptoms using standardized medical terminology. One person might describe feeling unusually cold, another might mention constant chills, while a clinician could categorize those experiences using a specific medical term.

Researchers therefore need a way to translate everyday language into standardized categories. One important reference is the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA), which provides terminology widely used to classify medical conditions, symptoms, and adverse events.

Previously, connecting huge numbers of informal social media posts with standardized medical terminology required enormous amounts of work, limiting how much data researchers could realistically analyze.

Large language models such as GPT and Gemini are changing that equation by allowing researchers to process and categorize vast amounts of text more consistently and quickly.

"Large language models have made it possible to do this kind of analysis much faster with a level of standardization that could be difficult to achieve before," says Sehgal.

Unexpected Symptoms Emerge From 400,000 Posts

The researchers stress that Reddit users do not represent the overall population of people taking GLP-1 drugs. Reddit users tend to be younger, are more likely to be male, and are disproportionately located in the United States.

Even with that limitation, the analysis produced a reassuring sign that the approach was detecting genuine patterns. Many of the symptoms discussed by Reddit users closely matched already known effects of semaglutide and tirzepatide.

About 44% of users included in the study described at least one side effect. Gastrointestinal problems were the most common, consistent with the nausea and other digestive issues already associated with these medications.

More intriguing were symptoms that appeared frequently enough to attract the researchers' attention but may not be as well represented in current drug labels or conventional adverse event reports.

Nearly 4% of users who reported side effects described reproductive symptoms. These included changes in menstruation such as bleeding between periods, heavy bleeding, and irregular menstrual cycles.

Users also described changes involving body temperature, including chills, feeling unusually cold, hot flashes, and symptoms resembling a fever.

Fatigue was another notable finding. It was the second most frequently reported complaint in the Reddit data, even though relatively few clinical trials reported fatigue often enough for it to reach established reporting thresholds.

Why Menstrual and Temperature Changes Are Interesting

One possible reason these reports caught researchers' attention involves the hypothalamus, a small but extremely important region of the brain. Among its many jobs, the hypothalamus helps regulate hunger, hormones, reproduction and body temperature.

"These drugs are thought to work by engaging part of the brain called the hypothalamus, which helps regulate a wide variety of hormones," says Jena Shaw Tronieri, Senior Research Investigator at Penn's Center for Weight and Eating Disorders and a co-author of the study. "That doesn't mean the medications are necessarily causing these symptoms, but it could suggest that reports of menstrual changes and body temperature fluctuations are worth studying more systematically."

The researchers are not proposing that this biological connection proves GLP-1 drugs are responsible. Instead, it offers another reason to test the patient-reported patterns more carefully through controlled research.

Turning Online Conversations Into Research Leads

For now, the team hopes the results encourage scientists and clinicians to pay closer attention to symptoms that patients repeatedly discuss online.

"They're clearly on patients' minds, and that's worth paying attention to," says Sehgal.

The researchers also want to broaden their analysis beyond Reddit and beyond English-language communities. Doing so could help determine whether the same patterns emerge among different groups of people and on different social media platforms.

"We don't really know yet whether what we're seeing on Reddit reflects the experience of GLP-1 users globally, or whether it's particular to the kind of person who posts on Reddit in the United States," Ungar says.

In the longer term, rapid AI analysis of online patient conversations could potentially become an early detection system for emerging health concerns involving drugs, supplements and wellness products.

That could be particularly valuable for substances that become popular online faster than conventional research can keep up. Loosely regulated or unregulated products, including injectable peptides, can spread quickly through communities on Reddit, TikTok and other platforms. Discussions among users may therefore provide some of the earliest indications of unexpected effects.

"The whole point of this kind of approach is that it can move quickly, and that's exactly when it's most valuable," says Guntuku.

This study was conducted at the University of Pennsylvania School of Engineering and Applied Science. The authors report no outside funding. Tronieri reports receiving an investigator-initiated grant, on behalf of the University of Pennsylvania, from Novo Nordisk and receiving consulting fees from Currax Pharmaceuticals, LLC. The other authors report no conflicts of interest.
